  ⎿  Interrupted · What should Claude do instead?

❯ Pause and explain your approach as you go. Tie the technical decisions you    
  make back to the requirements detailed in the project PDF.                    

⏺ Pausing to walk through what's built and why. Here's the mapping from the
  PDF's requirements to the decisions so far.

  Requirement 1 — the models

  The PDF specifies Job (name, created_at, updated_at) and JobStatus (job FK,
  status_type with ≥4 states, timestamp). I implemented those fields exactly,
  but the shape of the relationship is the real design decision.

  JobStatus is an append-only log, never mutated. The spec forces this read:
  PATCH /api/jobs/<id>/ is described as "creating a new JobStatus entry," and
  GET /api/jobs/ wants "the status_type from the latest JobStatus entry." So a
  job's current status is derived, not stored — and the same table becomes the
  audit history the "Job Status History View" stretch goal asks for, for free.
  Job.record_status() (backend/jobs/models.py:46) is the single write path, so
  that invariant can't be bypassed from a view or the admin.

  One wrinkle that falls out: updated_at uses auto_now, which only fires on
  Job.save(). A status-only change wouldn't touch it, which makes the field a
  lie for exactly the operation the dashboard performs most. record_status()
  updates it explicitly.

  Cascade delete on the FK satisfies "Ensure that all associated JobStatus
  entries are also deleted" at the database level rather than in view code.

  Requirement 5 — millions of jobs

  This is graded separately, so I front-loaded it into the schema rather than
  retrofitting.

  - (job, -timestamp, -id) index on JobStatus makes "latest status for this job"
    a one-row index scan, and also covers "full history for this job." Those
    are the only two ways that table is ever read.
  - (-created_at, -id) index on Job backs the list ordering.
  - with_current_status() uses a correlated subquery instead of a join +
    DISTINCT ON. A join materializes the latest status for every job before the
    limit applies; the subquery is only evaluated for the ~25 rows a page
    actually returns.
  - Cursor pagination (backend/jobs/pagination.py) rather than page numbers. Two
    reasons: OFFSET makes the database walk and discard every skipped row, so
    page 10,000 gets linearly slower; and PageNumberPagination issues a COUNT(*)
    per request, which is a full scan in PostgreSQL. The tradeoff is no total
    count and no jump-to-page — which pushes the frontend toward "load
    more"/infinite scroll. I think that's the right trade at this scale, and
    I'll write it up in the README.

  The -id tiebreaker on both orderings isn't cosmetic: keyset pagination needs a
  total ordering, and created_at alone can collide on bulk inserts.

  Requirement 4 — deployment

  Every environment-specific value reads from the environment (DATABASE_URL,
  allowed hosts, CORS origins), so the same image runs locally and under docker 
  compose with no code change. That's what makes the make build / make up path
  clean later.

  One thing to flag: I included a SQLite branch in the DATABASE_URL parser. The
  spec says configure for PostgreSQL, and Postgres is the default and the only
  thing compose will run — but Docker isn't installed on this machine, so SQLite
  is how I verify the suite as I go. If you'd rather keep the backend strictly
  Postgres-only, it's a five-line deletion; say the word.

  Requirement 2 — what the frontend needs

  The serializer I just wrote exposes status as a single symmetric field:
  clients read the same key they write. Writing it appends a history entry
  rather than overwriting a row. POST defaults to PENDING, which is the spec's
  "an initial JobStatus entry should be automatically created." name is
  validated server-side too, mirroring the client-side validation the spec asks
  for — client validation is UX, not a guarantee.

  JobDetailSerializer adds status_history on the detail route, which sets up two
  stretch goals (history view, details page with its own URL) without changing
  the list payload's size.

  ---

  not yet committed — next up are the viewset and URL routing, then I'll verify

✻ Churned for 24s · done 5:14 PM

❯ If we're treating JobStatus as append-only, will this be storing the statuses 
  in our DB indefinitely? What is our strategy for expiring this data, if       
  relevant                                                                      
⏺ Short answer: yes, unbounded today, and there's no expiry. That's a deliberate
  choice for this scope, but it's worth being precise about where it actually
  breaks.

  The bound that applies

  The key distinction is whether status rows are transitions or telemetry. Our
  model only writes on transition — record_status() is called from job creation
  and from PATCH. So a typical job accrues 2–4 rows across its whole lifecycle
  (PENDING → RUNNING → COMPLETED), and JobStatus grows as a small constant
  multiple of Job, not with time.

  That's very different from the failure mode people usually fear with
  append-only logs. If a real Rescale worker heartbeated "still RUNNING" every
  30 seconds, a single long job would emit thousands of rows and growth would be
  unbounded per job. Our write path can't do that. If it ever needed to —
  progress percentages, node-level telemetry — that belongs in a separate
  metrics table with its own retention, not in the transition log.

  The size math

  At 10M jobs × ~3 transitions = ~30M status rows. Each row is ~24 bytes of
  tuple header + 8 (id) + 8 (job_id) + 8 (timestamp) + ~10 (status_type), so ~60
  bytes, call it ~2 GB heap, plus roughly the same again for the composite
  index. Order of magnitude: ~4 GB. PostgreSQL does not care about a 4 GB table
  with a good index. The Job table and the frontend hit problems well before
  JobStatus does.

  So the honest answer to "what's the strategy" is: at the scale the PDF
  describes, none is required. The log is cheap, and it's the source of truth
  for the current status.

  Where it does break, and the ladder

  Retention becomes worth building when either the transition count per job
  stops being constant, or compliance requires deletion rather than permitting
  retention.

  The ordering I'd follow:

  1. Deletion already cascades. Removing a job removes its history. If jobs have
     a lifecycle policy, the log inherits it and you may need nothing else.
  2. Denormalize current_status onto Job first. This is a hard prerequisite, and
     it's the interesting coupling: today the latest row is load-bearing — it's
     what GET /api/jobs/ reads. You cannot prune history without a rule like
     "delete rows older than N days except each job's most recent," which is an
     expensive anti-join and easy to get wrong. Once Job.current_status is
     maintained in the same transaction as the append, JobStatus becomes purely
     historical and is safe to prune wholesale.
  3. Range-partition JobStatus by timestamp, monthly. Expiry then becomes DETACH
     PARTITION + DROP — constant time, no dead tuples, no vacuum pressure. A
     bulk DELETE of millions of rows is the wrong tool: it bloats the heap and
     the index and leaves autovacuum to clean up under load. Two caveats worth
     knowing up front: a partitioned table's primary key must include the
     partition key, so the PK becomes (id, timestamp); and Django has no native
     support for this, so it's a RunSQL migration or django-postgres-extra.
  4. Tier to cold storage before dropping, if the history has audit or analytics
     value — nightly export of the expiring partition to Parquet in object
     storage, then drop. Keeps the query path fast while keeping the record.

  Recommendation for this exercise

  Don't build any of it. Step 3 is a meaningful chunk of a 4-hour budget and
  would be speculative — and step 2 changes the schema in a way I'd rather
  justify with the filtering stretch goal than with a retention scenario nobody
  asked for.

  What I'd do instead is write this up in the README's performance section:
  state that the log is transition-bounded, give the size estimate, and name
  partitioning + the current_status denormalization as the path if the
  assumption changes. That demonstrates the thinking without spending the time,
  which is what the PDF's "briefly describe any performance considerations" is
  asking for.

  Want me to fold that into the README section when I get there, or would you
  rather I actually implement the denormalized current_status now? It's
  genuinely cheap — about ten lines and one migration — and it pays off twice,
  since it's also what makes filtering by status indexable for the stretch goal.

✻ Sautéed for 38s · done 5:17 PM

❯ Downsides of denormalizing current_status? Does this impact our other         
  priorities?                                                                   

⏺ Real downsides, and they're worth weighing carefully — because the win is
  narrower than it first looks.

  The read win on GET /api/jobs/ is small

  Worth saying first: the subquery is already only evaluated for the 25 rows a
  page returns. Denormalizing saves ~25 index lookups per request. That's noise.
  The list endpoint is not the reason to do this.

  The real win is filtering: WHERE status = 'RUNNING' ORDER BY created_at DESC 
  LIMIT 25. With an annotation, Postgres must compute the subquery for every row
  before it can filter — a full scan over millions. With a real column and a
  (current_status, -created_at, -id) index, it's a range scan. That's
  milliseconds vs. minutes. So the denormalization is justified by the filtering
  stretch goal, and barely at all by the core requirement.

  The costs

  Drift. The invariant moves from the database into application code. Any path
  that doesn't go through record_status() silently breaks it — a data migration,
  a fixture load, a future management command. There's already a live example:
  the JobStatusInline I put in backend/jobs/admin.py:6 lets an admin add a
  status row directly. That's completely safe today precisely because nothing is
  denormalized. The moment we add the column, that inline becomes a correctness
  bug.

  A lost-update race. Two concurrent transitions on one job both append rows
  correctly — the log stays right. But the two UPDATEs on Job land in whatever
  order they commit, not in timestamp order. You can end up with current_status 
  = RUNNING while the latest log row says COMPLETED. Fixing it properly means
  SELECT FOR UPDATE on the job row, or a conditional update guarded by a second
  current_status_at column. With the subquery this is impossible by
  construction, because "latest" is computed, never stored.

  It's a cache, so it needs a repair path. Realistically that means a management
  command to recompute from the log — more code, and something you'd genuinely
  want in production.

  It adds a way to be wrong. The PDF grades Correctness and Maintainability, and
  says to be ready to explain and extend the solution. A derived field that can
  go stale is exactly what a reviewer probes in a follow-up.

  What it doesn't cost

  The write is already paid for — record_status() updates the Job row anyway for
  updated_at, so we add a column to an existing UPDATE, not a new one. And the
  API contract is unchanged: status reads and writes identically, so the
  frontend never knows. It's a backend-internal, reversible change.

  Recommendation

  Defer it. Ship the subquery, and add the column only if we implement status
  filtering. Honest estimate if we do: 30–45 minutes once concurrency handling,
  invariant tests, and fixing that admin inline are included — not the ten
  minutes I claimed earlier. That's real budget against a stretch goal while
  make test still has to pass.

  And if we do add it, I'd lean toward a database trigger over application code.
  For an append-only log the logic is four lines of PL/pgSQL, and it covers
  every write path — admin, fixtures, raw SQL — while also resolving the
  ordering race in one place. The cost is logic that lives in a RunSQL migration
  where Django developers won't expect it.

  Shall I carry on with the viewset and URL routing?

✻ Brewed for 50s · done 5:21 PM

❯ Keep in mind scalability, we want to be building this with millions of        
  concurrent jobs in mind                                                       

⏺ Worth separating two different claims, because they stress different parts of
  the system and only one of them is what I've designed for so far.

  Millions of rows stored is a read/indexing problem — that's what cursor
  pagination and the composite indexes address. Millions of concurrent jobs is a
  write-throughput and working-set problem, and it changes the primary access
  pattern.

  What actually changes

  If a million jobs are RUNNING right now, nobody opens a list sorted by
  creation date and scrolls. Every meaningful view is filtered by status — "show
  me what's running," "show me today's failures." That moves status filtering
  from a stretch goal to the dominant query.

  And that flips my recommendation from the last answer. I argued for deferring
  the denormalized current_status because filtering was optional. Under this
  requirement it isn't, and the subquery annotation is exactly wrong for it:
  Postgres must evaluate the annotation for every row before it can filter, so
  WHERE status = 'RUNNING' degrades to a full scan over the whole table. A real
  column with a (current_status, created_at DESC, id DESC) index turns the same
  query into a range scan that also satisfies the cursor ordering. That's the 
  change I'd make now.

  The contention worry I raised turns out not to apply, and it's worth being
  explicit about why: maintaining the column takes a row lock on the Job row,
  but transitions are ~3 per job per lifetime. Concurrency is across millions of
  distinct jobs, so the locks never collide. The lock is for correctness —
  preventing the lost update where current_status lands out of order — not a
  throughput ceiling.

  How I'd maintain it

  select_for_update() on the job row inside record_status(), which already runs
  in a transaction. Roughly ten lines, and it stays portable.

  I'd previously leaned toward a database trigger. I'm backing off that for a
  concrete reason: a PL/pgSQL trigger is Postgres-only, and Docker isn't
  installed on this machine, so the only way I can currently run the suite is
  the SQLite path. A trigger would make the backend untestable here. The trigger
  is the right production hardening — it covers writes that never go through
  Django — and I'll note it in the README as such.

  The rest of the write path, honestly

  Three things matter at that rate that I do not propose building:

  - Hot-row update pressure. Every transition is an INSERT plus an UPDATE on
    Job, and each UPDATE leaves a dead tuple. At sustained high throughput
    that's autovacuum tuning and fillfactor, not application code.
  - Connection limits. Millions of concurrent producers will exhaust Postgres
    connections long before the query planner struggles; that's PgBouncer's job.
    One concrete follow-on: I set CONN_MAX_AGE=60, which is right for direct
    connections but should be 0 behind a transaction-mode pooler. Worth a README
    note.
  - Status counts. Dashboard tiles like "1.2M running" can't be COUNT(*). That's
    a rollup table or an approximate count. Cursor pagination already keeps us
    from paying it on the list endpoint.

  Realistically, status updates at that volume would arrive on a queue as
  batched writes rather than as individual PATCH calls — but the PDF specifies
  the REST contract, so that's a README paragraph, not a rewrite.

  Proposal

  Add current_status to Job maintained in record_status() under
  select_for_update(), plus the composite index, and support a ?status= filter
  on the list endpoint. About 40 minutes including tests, and it keeps the API
  contract identical so the frontend is unaffected. Everything above stays in
  the README's performance section.

  One thing that would help: is Docker available to you, or can I assume a local
  Postgres? Right now I can only verify against SQLite, which is why I'm
  avoiding Postgres-specific constructs like the trigger and partial indexes. If
  I can run the real database, I'd rather validate the index actually gets used
  with EXPLAIN than assert it in a comment.

  Shall I implement that and get back to the viewset?

⏺ Docker's daemon is up (server 29.8.0) — the CLI just isn't symlinked into
  PATH, it lives at /Applications/Docker.app/Contents/Resources/bin/docker. So I
  can run a real Postgres and validate the index with EXPLAIN rather than
  asserting it in a comment.

  Here's the full change set for filtering. No edits until you green-light it.

  1. models.py — add the current_status column

  current_status = models.CharField(
      max_length=16, choices=JobStatus.StatusType.choices, null=True, blank=True
  )

  Function: stores the status_type of the job's most recent history entry.
  Motivation: this is the whole point of the exercise — WHERE current_status = 
  'RUNNING' can use an index, where the correlated subquery could not. Nullable
  rather than defaulting to PENDING because a job with no history genuinely has
  no status, and I'd rather represent that than have the column assert something
  false.

  2. models.py — composite index

  models.Index(fields=("current_status", "-created_at", "-id"),
  name="job_status_created_idx")

  Function: backs WHERE current_status = ? ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC 
  LIMIT 25.
  Motivation: equality on the leading column plus the cursor's sort order as the
  trailing columns means Postgres walks a contiguous index range and stops at
  25 — no sort, no scan. The existing (-created_at, -id) index stays for the
  unfiltered list. This is the index I want to confirm with EXPLAIN.

  3. models.py — maintain the column in record_status()

  @transaction.atomic
  def record_status(self, status_type, timestamp=None):
      Job.objects.select_for_update().filter(pk=self.pk).exists()   # serialize 
  per-job transitions
      status = self.statuses.create(...)
      current = self.latest_status()                                # recompute,
  don't assume
      Job.objects.filter(pk=self.pk).update(current_status=current,
  updated_at=timezone.now())

  Function: appends the history row and refreshes the cached column in one
  transaction.
  Motivation: two decisions worth flagging. The row lock prevents the lost
  update I described — two concurrent transitions whose UPDATEs commit out of
  order, leaving current_status disagreeing with the log. It's contention-free
  in practice because it only serializes transitions of the same job, and a job
  sees ~3 in its lifetime. And it recomputes from the log rather than assuming
  the row just written is the newest, so a backdated timestamp can't corrupt the
  cache. Cost is one index lookup on the index we already have.

  4. models.py — delete with_current_status()

  Function: removes the subquery annotation.
  Motivation: not optional — Django raises The annotation 'current_status' 
  conflicts with a field on the model if both exist. The column replaces it.

  5. serializers.py — simplify CurrentStatusField

  Drop the latest_status() fallback; read the column.
  Motivation: the fallback would fire on every statusless job and issue a wasted
  query per row. If the cache can drift, the answer is a repair command, not a
  per-request re-query that silently hides the drift.

  6. admin.py — make the history inline read-only

  Motivation: this is the bug I flagged earlier, now live. JobStatusInline
  writes JobStatus rows directly, bypassing record_status(), so an admin edit
  would desync the column. Making the history read-only also matches the
  append-only model. current_status becomes a real sortable column and a
  list_filter.

  7. views.py — ?status= filter

  Hand-rolled validation against StatusType.choices, returning 400 with a clear
  message on an unknown value, rather than adding django-filter for a single
  parameter.
  Motivation: fewer dependencies to justify, and it keeps me in control of which
  params are allowed to hit the database — an unindexed filter param is how
  this kind of endpoint quietly regresses.

  8. Migration

  AddField + AddIndex + a RunPython that backfills current_status from the log.
  Motivation: the backfill is a no-op on our empty database, but it makes the
  migration correct if it's ever run against real data, and it's the same logic
  a repair command would use.

  One question

  Now that Postgres is available, should I drop the SQLite branch from 
  settings.py? It only existed so I could run tests without a database server.
  Tests will now run against the same engine as production, which is what you
  want for index-sensitive work — and the PDF does say to configure for
  PostgreSQL. It's a five-line deletion.

  Green-light this as-is, or tell me what to change?

❯ We're caching the latest status in the Job model, how stale can this get and  
  when is it refreshed?                                                         

⏺ Let me answer that precisely against the code as written.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Good news from the grep: there is exactly one write path.
  job.statuses.create(...) appears once, at backend/jobs/models.py:67, inside
  record_status(), and its only two callers are serializers.py:37 and
  serializers.py:48.

  When it refreshes

  One place, record_status() (backend/jobs/models.py:55), and the refresh
  happens inside the same transaction as the insert:

  job = Job.objects.select_for_update().get(pk=self.pk)   # lock
  status = job.statuses.create(...)                        # append to log
  job.current_status = job.latest_status()                 # recompute from log
  job.save(update_fields=("current_status", "updated_at")) # commit both 
  together

  How stale it can get: zero, on that path

  This is worth stating plainly because "cache" usually implies a staleness
  window, and here there isn't one. Because the insert and the cache update
  commit atomically, any other transaction sees both or neither. There is no
  instant at which the log has advanced and current_status hasn't. It's a
  transactionally-maintained denormalization, not an eventually-consistent one.

  The select_for_update closes the concurrency case: two simultaneous
  transitions on the same job can't interleave. The second blocks on the lock,
  and when it proceeds its latest_status() recompute sees the first one's
  committed row. And because it recomputes rather than assuming the row it just
  wrote is newest, a backdated timestamp also lands correctly.

  If save() fails, the whole transaction rolls back — including the status row.
  No half-written state.

  Where it can drift

  Three vectors, none currently live:

  1. Writes that bypass record_status(). Nothing enforces the discipline — the
  column is only correct because every writer goes through that method. A future
  management command, a data migration, a loaddata fixture, or raw SQL would
  desync it silently. This was why I made the admin inline read-only; that was a
  real instance of this hole.

  2. In-memory instances. Distinct from database staleness. If you hold a Job
  object and someone else transitions it, your job.current_status attribute is
  stale until refresh_from_db(). record_status() syncs the calling instance
  (self.current_status = job.current_status), but nothing else. This is ordinary
  Django behavior, and it's the one most likely to surprise someone writing
  tests.

  3. Bulk ingest — and this is the one that matters at your scale.
  record_status() is one transaction and one row lock per transition. If status
  updates arrive from a queue as batched writes rather than individual PATCH
  calls, nobody will call this method a thousand times per batch; they'll write
  set-based SQL. That path would have to maintain the cache itself, and it's
  exactly where the invariant gets dropped.

  What closes them

  Vector 3 is the argument for the database trigger I set aside earlier. A
  trigger maintains the column regardless of how rows arrive — ORM, bulk COPY,
  raw SQL — which is the only approach that survives a batched ingest path. Now
  that Postgres is confirmed available and I'm no longer constrained to portable
  constructs, that objection is gone. I'd still not build it today, because
  there's no bulk ingest path to protect yet.

  What I would add is a repair command — manage.py recompute_job_statuses, a
  batched UPDATE ... FROM that rebuilds the column from the log. About fifteen
  lines. It's the answer to "we think it drifted," and it's what you'd run after
  any bulk import. It also gives the invariant something to assert against in a
  backend test.

  Want me to add that before I commit, or note it in the README and keep this
  commit to what you've already reviewed? I'd lean toward adding it — a cache
  with no repair path is the part a reviewer will poke at.

✻ Baked for 5m 1s · done 5:33 PM

❯ What customer focusing issue would that solve? Is this "cache" drift          
  something to worry about in a theoretical prod environment?                   

⏺ Honestly? None today. You're right to push.

  Drift requires a write path that bypasses record_status(), and there isn't one
  — the grep confirmed a single call site. So a repair command would ship to
  fix a condition the code cannot currently produce. That's speculative work,
  and I was arguing for it on engineering tidiness rather than customer impact.

  What drift would actually cost, if it happened

  Worth being concrete rather than dismissing it. current_status is what the
  list renders and filters on, while the detail view reads status_history from
  the log. So drift doesn't show up as "the dashboard is wrong" — it shows up as
  the list and the detail page contradicting each other in the same session,
  which is worse for trust than being uniformly wrong.

  The version that actually hurts a Rescale-shaped customer: a job that FAILED
  still showing as RUNNING in the list, so nobody investigates and compute keeps
  burning against a dead run. Or "show me today's failures" quietly omitting
  one.

  Why the severity is bounded anyway

  The log is the source of truth and is never lost. Drift is a stale cache over
  durable data, not a lossy write — every drifted row is reconstructible,
  exactly, at any later time. And the recovery is a single UPDATE ... FROM 
  (SELECT DISTINCT ON (job_id) ...), which an engineer can write in five minutes
  on the day it's needed. Pre-building that for an event that has never
  occurred is inventory, not insurance.

  Where the guard does belong

  If the risk is "someone later adds a second write path," then the thing that
  catches it is a test, not a runtime command — asserting current_status == 
  latest_status() after each transition. That fails in CI the moment someone
  introduces a bypassing write, which is when it costs nothing to fix. A repair
  command only helps after the bad data already reached a customer.

  That's also a better use of the budget: backend unit tests are an explicit
  stretch goal in the PDF, so the assertion earns its place twice.

  When it stops being theoretical

  The moment a non-ORM write path exists — the batched queue ingest we
  discussed. At that point you want the trigger and a backfill, and they ship
  together, because the backfill exists to fix rows written before the trigger
  did. That's the natural home for this work, and it isn't now.

  Recommendation: drop it. I'll fold the invariant assertion into the backend
  tests and note the trigger-plus-backfill path in the README's performance
  section.

  Back to where you stopped me — you've seen settings.py, models.py, admin.py,
  and the migration. Still unreviewed are the three API files (serializers.py,
  views.py, jobs/urls.py) and the trimmed config/urls.py. Want me to print
  those, or have you seen enough to let me commit the three groups?

  One flag on the migration you did see: I regenerated 0001_initial rather than
  stacking an 0002_add_current_status on top. Cleaner history for a greenfield
  repo, but it does rewrite a migration that's already in commit 4947d45. Say
  the word if you'd rather I stack it — that's the habit you'd want in
  production.

✻ Brewed for 37s · done 5:36 PM

❯ Let's assume we won't have non-ORM write paths for the scope of the project.  
  Is it accurate to say Job.current_status doesn't "cache" the newest entry's   
  state, but stores it?                                                         

⏺ Yes — that's accurate, and the distinction is load-bearing rather than
  pedantic.

  A cache implies a staleness window, a miss path, and a reader that may need to
  fall back to the source. current_status has none of those: it's written in
  the same transaction as the log entry, so no reader ever observes the two
  disagreeing. Under the stipulation of ORM-only writes, this holds as an
  invariant, not as a probability:

  job.current_status == newest(job.statuses).status_type    # always, for any 
  observer

  The right term is a derived or denormalized column — the same category as a
  running total kept on an invoice header. It's redundant (recomputable from the
  log) without being unreliable.

  Both things stay true at once, and it's worth keeping them straight: the log
  remains the source of truth for the job's history, while current_status is the
  materialized answer to one specific question. If they ever disagreed, the log
  would win — but the transaction is what guarantees they don't.

  Why the wrong noun is actively harmful here: "cache" invites the next reader
  to think "this might be stale, should I fall back to the log?" — which is
  exactly the per-row fallback query I deliberately deleted from the serializer.
  The comments would be arguing against the design.

  Wording I'd change (docstrings only, no behavior)

  - models.py:5 — "Job.current_status caches the newest entry's state" → "holds
    the newest entry's state, maintained transactionally with the log"
  - models.py:56 — "refresh the cached current_status" → "update current_status"
  - models.py:59 — "so the cache cannot drift from the log" → "so the column and
    the log always agree"
  - models.py:84 — "current_status is the cached answer... keeps that cache
    honest" → frame latest_status() as the definition of the invariant, which is
    what record_status() maintains and what a test can assert against
  - admin.py:9 — "caches its newest entry... desync that cache" → "holds its
    newest entry... break that invariant"

  A real bug this reframing exposed

  Making the invariant claim precise made me check whether it actually holds at
  creation, and it doesn't quite. In serializers.py:35-37:

  job = Job.objects.create(**validated_data)   # transaction 1 — commits alone
  job.record_status(status_type)               # transaction 2

  record_status() is atomic, but the Job insert isn't inside it. So there's a
  committed window where a job exists with current_status = NULL and no history
  — briefly visible in the list — and if record_status() raises, that job is
  left permanently statusless. The PDF explicitly requires that creating a job
  automatically creates its initial status entry; right now that's two
  transactions, not one.

  Fix is one decorator: @transaction.atomic on JobSerializer.create(), so the
  job and its opening status commit together or not at all.

  Shall I apply the wording changes and that atomicity fix? Both are small, and
  I'd rather they land before you review the API files than as a follow-up.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Confirmed — a real bug, not a test artifact. The response is ~8ms behind the
  database: it reports the updated_at loaded before the write, so a POST returns
  a job whose updated_at predates its own initial status entry.

  Root cause is the two-instance split I introduced: record_status() locks and
  saves a different Python object (job), then hand-syncs only current_status
  back to self. updated_at never makes the trip. Fix is to drop the second
  instance entirely:

  sessions.

  One thing I noticed while verifying indexes

  Django auto-created jobs_jobstatus_job_id_e6419813 on the FK, but
  jobstatus_latest_idx already leads with job_id, so it serves every lookup the
  single-column index would — including the cascade-delete constraint check. The
  FK index is pure write amplification: an extra index write on every status
  insert, which is the hottest write path in the system.

  Setting db_index=False on the FK would drop it. It's a one-line model change
  plus a migration, and it's the same class of reasoning that led me to drop
  job_name_idx. Want it, or leave it?

  What's left from the brief

  Backend core is done. Remaining: the React/TypeScript frontend, Dockerfiles
  and docker-compose.yml, the Makefile, the Playwright E2E test, and the README
  with the performance write-up (I have real EXPLAIN numbers to put in it — the
  0.053 ms vs 76.8 ms keyset-vs-offset comparison and the 19.9 ms COUNT(*) that
  cursor pagination avoids).

  Worth flagging on sequencing: make test is a hard gate — the brief says if it
  fails they stop evaluating. So I'd get Docker Compose and the Makefile working
  end to end before polishing the frontend, rather than leaving the packaging
  to last. Also still outstanding is the backend test asserting the
  current_status == latest_status() invariant, which I said I'd fold in.

  Where do you want to go next — frontend, or the Docker/Makefile gate first?

❯ DB state looks sane after I verified manually. Do these tests need to clean   
  up state after running?                                                       
  ⎿  ⧉ Selected 1 lines from backend/jobs/views.py in Visual Studio Code

⏺ No — cleanup is automatic, at two layers. Let me prove it rather than assert
  it:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Proof: dev database identical before and after (5 jobs, 12 statuses), and
  test_jobs no longer exists.

  Two layers, both automatic

  Per run — Django creates test_jobs before the suite and drops it after (that's
  the "Destroying test database" line). Your jobs database is never touched;
  it's a different database entirely, not just different rows.

  Per test — TestCase wraps each test method in a transaction and rolls it back
  in teardown. No test observes another's data, and nothing accumulates across
  the run.

  So no tearDown is needed anywhere, and a Job.objects.all().delete() inside a
  test would be dead code. That's also why the suite runs in 0.24s — rollback is
  far cheaper than truncation.

  One thing rollback does not reset

  Sequences. Primary keys keep climbing across tests within a run — that's why
  the verbose output showed /api/jobs/16/ and /api/jobs/18/ rather than low
  numbers. Harmless, but it's why none of my tests hardcode an ID; they all use
  the id the API returned. A test asserting id == 1 would pass in isolation and
  fail in suite order.

  An honest limitation

  Because TestCase runs each test inside an enclosing transaction, the
  select_for_update() in record_status() never actually contends for a lock. The
  suite proves the logic is correct — including that the value is recomputed
  rather than assumed — but it does not prove the locking behaves under real
  concurrency.

  Proving that needs TransactionTestCase plus threads on separate connections,
  which also changes cleanup to table truncation and is substantially slower. My
  view: not worth the budget here. It's a standard pattern, and the race it
  guards against requires two transitions on one job within milliseconds. I'd
  note it in the README as tested-by-inspection rather than build a threaded
  test.

  Worth separating from the above: my earlier manual verification scripts did
  write to the dev database, and I cleaned up after each. The 5 jobs you
  inspected are deliberate seed data, not test residue.

  ---

  Shall I commit the tests and move on to Docker Compose and the Makefile?
  That's the make test gate, so I'd aim to have the full stack — Postgres,
  Django, and the backend suite — running end to end in containers before the
  frontend exists, then slot Playwright in once there's a UI to drive.

❯ Why are you using port 5433 instead of 5432?                                  

⏺ Honest answer: I chose it defensively, and the specific reason I chose it
  doesn't apply on this machine.

  It's a host-side mapping only — "5433:5432". Inside the Compose network the
  backend still connects to db:5432 (see DATABASE_URL: 
  postgres://jobs:jobs@db:5432/jobs). Nothing about Postgres itself is
  non-standard; 5433 is purely which host port exposes the container.

  I picked it when I started the scratch container, on the assumption you might
  have a local Postgres on 5432. I'd actually already checked and found none
  installed here, so on your machine 5432 would have been free. I then carried
  5433 into Compose so your pgAdmin connection wouldn't have to change
  mid-stream.

  The reason it's still arguably right

  The machine that matters isn't yours — it's the evaluator's. The brief says
  they run make test first and stop evaluating if it fails. A developer with
  Postgres already running on 5432 is common, and a published-port collision
  fails docker compose up outright with port is already allocated. That's a hard
  failure on the one command that gates everything.

  The same exposure exists on 8000, which is if anything more commonly occupied.

  Options

  1. Change to 5432 — least surprise, matches every default, but reintroduces
     the collision risk on the evaluator's machine.
  2. Keep 5433, document it — safe, mildly surprising.
  3. Make both host ports overridable with safe defaults —
     "${POSTGRES_HOST_PORT:-5433}:5432" and "${BACKEND_HOST_PORT:-8000}:8000".
     Collision-resistant by default, and anyone who wants the conventional ports
     sets one variable.

  I'd go with 3: it costs two lines, protects the hard gate, and gives you 5432
  whenever you want it via POSTGRES_HOST_PORT=5432 make up. Worth noting the
  frontend will need the backend's host port too, since the browser calls the
  API from outside the Compose network — so that variable will get referenced
  again shortly.

  Which do you want?

  For completeness on where I'd got to: make build, up, test, stop are all
  verified working — the stack comes up healthy, /api/health/ returns ok, the
  API serves, and 27 tests pass in-container in ~24s including the build. Still
  to verify is make clean, plus a from-scratch clean → test run to prove the
  evaluator's path, and reseeding your pgAdmin data.

⏺ Committed as 62af91d. Here's how to verify all of it yourself.

  First: docker isn't on your PATH

  That's why every command I ran was prefixed with an export. Docker Desktop is
  installed and its daemon is running, but the CLI symlink into /usr/local/bin
  is missing, so a bare make test in your terminal will fail with docker: 
  command not found.

  Either export it per-session:

  export PATH="/Applications/Docker.app/Contents/Resources/bin:$PATH"

  or fix it permanently — Docker Desktop → Settings → Advanced → enable CLI
  tools installation, or symlink it yourself. Worth doing, since the evaluator's
  machine will have it on PATH and I'd rather your local runs match theirs.

  In this session you can prefix with ! and the output lands in our conversation
  — e.g. ! make test.

❯ What is the Node.js version problem?                                          
⏺ Let me check the actual requirement rather than assert it:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Two layers to it.

  The immediate error: create-vite imports styleText from node:util. That
  function landed in Node 20.12.0 and was backported to 21.7.0. Your host is
  21.6.2 — just before that backport. So despite 21 > 20.12, this specific build
  genuinely lacks the export, and the ESM import fails at parse time. That's
  the SyntaxError: The requested module 'node:util' does not provide an export 
  named 'styleText'.

  The underlying issue: the whole toolchain declares the same engine range:

  vite@8                 ^20.19.0 || >=22.12.0
  @vitejs/plugin-react@6 ^20.19.0 || >=22.12.0
  create-vite            ^20.19.0 || >=22.12.0

  Node 21.6.2 satisfies neither branch — ^20.19.0 caps below 21.0.0, and 21.6.2
  is under 22.12.0. Node 21 is an odd-numbered "Current" line, which never gets
  LTS and went end-of-life in June 2024. The range excludes it deliberately, so
  this isn't a near-miss I can paper over.

  Does it matter?

  For the deliverable, no. The frontend builds and runs inside Docker on a
  pinned Node image, and the brief guarantees the evaluator has only make,
  docker, docker compose, and bash. Host Node never enters the picture — which
  is exactly why I scaffolded in node:22-alpine rather than working around it.

  For your local machine, only if you want to run Vite directly. npm install && 
  npm run dev on the host will fail. If you'd like the local dev server with hot
  reload, install Node 22 LTS (nvm install 22 && nvm use 22). I don't need it —
  I'll do frontend work through the container either way.

  I'll pin the Node version in the frontend Dockerfile so builds are
  reproducible regardless of anyone's host, and I'd suggest adding a .nvmrc so
  the required version is recorded rather than tribal knowledge. Say if you'd
  rather skip that.

  Continuing with the scaffold now — stripping the Vite demo boilerplate, wiring
  the dev-server and nginx /api proxies, and adding the frontend service to
  Compose. Then I'll bring you the UI discussion with the stack proven end to
  end.

✻ Baked for 3m 8s · done 6:11 PM

❯ Continue with the scaffolding. Make sure to have me review every commit

❯ What is the change you made to ApiError supposed to accomplish?               

⏺ Full stack is up: nginx serves the built app on :5173 and proxies /api to
  Django same-origin — the seeded jobs come back through it.

  What the ApiError change accomplishes

  At runtime, nothing — the two versions are behaviorally identical. It was
  purely to make the build compile.

  My first version used TypeScript's constructor parameter properties:

  constructor(message: string, readonly status: number) { ... }

  That shorthand implicitly generates this.status = status. It's sugar that
  emits runtime code rather than being a pure type annotation, and the tsconfig
  create-vite generated enables erasableSyntaxOnly, which bans exactly that
  category — parameter properties, enum, runtime namespace, import =. Hence
  error TS1294.

  The point of that flag is that all remaining TypeScript syntax can be erased
  to valid JavaScript by simply deleting annotations. That's what lets Node run
  .ts files directly via type-stripping, and it keeps tools like esbuild from
  having to synthesize code. A stripper can only remove; it can't invent the
  assignment a parameter property implies.

  So my rewrite just writes out what the sugar was doing — explicit field
  declarations plus explicit assignment. readonly itself survives, because it's
  a type-level modifier that erases cleanly; only the shorthand was the problem.

  To be straight about it: this wasn't a design decision, it was me reaching for
  a convenience syntax that this project's config forbids. The alternative
  would be turning erasableSyntaxOnly off, but I'd keep it — it's the modern
  default, it costs three lines, and it keeps the type-stripping option open.

  Ready to commit — please review

  New frontend/ scaffold plus a Compose change. You've seen each file's contents
  as I wrote them; the set is:

  - frontend/ — package.json, package-lock.json, index.html, .nvmrc, .gitignore,
    .oxlintrc.json, tsconfigs
  - frontend/vite.config.ts — dev-server /api proxy, host: true
  - frontend/src/api/types.ts, client.ts — typed API layer, ApiError with
    field-level detail
  - frontend/src/App.tsx — placeholder shell, replaced by the real dashboard
  - frontend/src/index.css, main.tsx, public/favicon.svg
  - frontend/Dockerfile, nginx.conf, .dockerignore — multi-stage build → nginx
  - docker-compose.yml — frontend service, healthcheck on 127.0.0.1

  Two decisions worth your attention:

  Same-origin /api proxy. The app calls the relative path /api; the dev server
  and nginx both proxy it to Django. This means no CORS anywhere, and no backend
  URL baked into the bundle — Vite fixes env vars at build time, so an absolute
  URL would hardcode the deploy target into the image. The browser only ever
  talks to :5173.

  Frontend on 5173, backend still on 8000. The browser no longer needs 8000, but
  I left it published for direct API access and the admin.

  Want the full git diff before I commit, or shall I commit and move to the UI?

  the EC2/Fargate console model you mentioned, and there are a few real forks

❯ what's with the localhost vs. 127.0.0.1 mapping in docker-compose.yml? could  
  this cause compatibility issues on an evaluator's machine                     

⏺ Good question — let me answer it precisely, because there's a related issue in
  a different healthcheck that your question surfaces.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Confirmed — and your question surfaced a real latent issue in the backend
  healthcheck.

  The frontend 127.0.0.1 is the safe choice, not a risk

  That probe runs inside the container's own network namespace: wget 
  http://127.0.0.1/ never touches the host's network stack. Every Linux
  container gets a lo interface with 127.0.0.1/8 configured, regardless of the
  host OS, Docker version, or host IPv6 configuration. There's nothing for an
  evaluator's machine to differ on.

  localhost is the fragile one. The container's /etc/hosts maps it to both
  127.0.0.1 and ::1, getaddrinfo returns ::1 first (just verified above), and
  nginx binds IPv4 only — so BusyBox wget, which doesn't retry the next address,
  gets refused and the container is marked unhealthy.

  Why I didn't fix it at nginx instead

  The tempting fix is listen [::]:80; so localhost works either way. That's
  worse: in a container without IPv6, nginx fails to bind and exits with Address
  family not supported by protocol. That turns a healthcheck quirk into a hard
  boot failure on a machine I can't inspect. Pinning the probe to IPv4 keeps the
  failure mode impossible.

  The actual finding: the backend healthcheck

  It uses localhost, and the test above shows why that's been quietly working:

  getaddrinfo('localhost', 8000) → ::1 first, then 127.0.0.1
  direct ::1 attempt              → refused: [Errno 111]
  urllib via 'localhost'          → succeeded in 5.8 ms

  Python's socket.create_connection iterates every address from getaddrinfo and
  falls back to IPv4, so it recovers. But every 5-second probe burns a refused
  IPv6 connection first, and the check only passes because of that retry
  behavior — swap the probe for curl, or run it in an image with a stricter
  client, and it breaks exactly like the nginx one did.

  Same class of bug, currently masked. I'd make it explicit:

  docker-compose.yml diff is above; the 19 new frontend/ files are the scaffold

  build(frontend): scaffold React/TypeScript app behind a same-origin API proxy

  Vite + React 19 + TypeScript, generated on node:22-alpine because the host
  Node (21.6.2) falls outside the toolchain's ^20.19 || >=22.12 range, with the
  demo boilerplate stripped and the Node version recorded in .nvmrc.

  The app calls the relative path /api, which the dev server and the production
  nginx image both proxy to Django. That makes every environment same-origin, so
  CORS never applies and no backend URL is baked into the bundle — Vite fixes 
  env
  vars at build time, which would otherwise hardcode the deploy target.

  Serves the built assets from nginx with immutable caching on hashed assets and
  an index.html fallback so client-side routes are not 404s.

  Both HTTP healthchecks probe 127.0.0.1 rather than localhost: containers
  resolve localhost to ::1 first while both servers bind IPv4 only, so nginx's
  wget was refused outright and gunicorn's check passed only because Python
  retries the next address.

  src/App.tsx is a placeholder proving the wiring, replaced by the dashboard.

  Want the full frontend diff first, or is that enough to commit?

  ---

  UI discussion

  Taking the EC2-instances / Fargate-tasks console as the model: a dense table,
  status as a badge, a toolbar with filter and primary action, row-level
  actions, and a detail view behind its own URL. Here are the forks I think are
  genuinely yours to call.

  1. Dense table, not cards. Matches the reference, scans better, and keeps the
  "millions of jobs" story coherent — cards waste vertical space and can't be
  virtualized as naturally. Recommend: table.

  2. Status changes inline, not buried in an action menu. AWS hides "Stop
  instance" behind a menu plus confirmation because it's expensive and
  disruptive. Our status change is an append to an audit log — cheap,
  non-destructive, fully reversible, and it never loses information. That
  asymmetry means it doesn't need the ceremony. Recommend: an inline dropdown 
  per row; delete gets the confirmation instead, since it genuinely destroys 
  history.

  3. Pagination: Next/Previous, not infinite scroll. This is where the backend
  constrains the UI. Cursor pagination has no total, so "1–25 of 1,234" is
  impossible without a COUNT(*) we deliberately avoid. Infinite scroll would
  also grow the DOM without bound, which quietly contradicts the scalability
  argument unless I add virtualization. Recommend: Next/Prev driven by the 
  server's cursors, with a page-size control — bounded DOM, honest about the 
  semantics.

  4. Status filter as tabs, server-side. All / Pending / Running / Completed / 
  Failed across the top, hitting ?status= and resetting the cursor. Never filter
  client-side — that would only filter the current page and look broken. Caveat
  worth your call: console tabs usually show counts, and we can't produce those
  without either a COUNT(*) per state or a rollup. Recommend: no counts for 
  now; note the rollup option in the README.

  5. Detail view at its own route. The stretch goal explicitly asks for "a
  separate page with a distinct URL," so /jobs/:id with the status history as a
  timeline, rather than a side drawer. Costs a react-router dependency.
  Recommend: do it — it satisfies the stretch goal literally and the detail 
  serializer already returns the history.

  6. Manual refresh, not polling. Real jobs change state server-side, so the
  list goes stale. A refresh button plus optimistic updates after mutations
  covers it without background noise. Recommend: button now; polling is a small 
  addition if you want it.

  7. Styling: hand-written CSS with custom-property tokens + CSS Modules. No
  extra dependency, full control over the console aesthetic, and scoping per
  component. Tailwind would mean another build integration for a nine-component
  app. Recommend: tokens + modules.

  Throughout I'd keep status badges carrying text as well as colour (colour
  alone fails for colour-blind users and in screenshots), real <th> headers, and
  an aria-live region for errors.

  Happy to just proceed on all seven recommendations — or tell me which to flip.
  The ones I'd most expect you to push back on are 2 (inline vs. deliberate
  action menu) and 3 (Next/Prev vs. load-more).

⏺ Now verifying the local dev path actually works with your Node 24:

  Ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes — it runs locally, verified end to end with your Node 24. Both paths work
  from the same config.

  How local dev works

  The /api proxy design pays off here: the app calls the relative path in both
  modes, so nothing changes between them.

  make dev                                  # db + backend in Docker, frontend 
  container stopped
  cd frontend && nvm use && npm install && npm run dev

  Vite serves on :5173 and proxies /api → http://localhost:8000. Verified: dev
  server up in 95ms, localhost:5173/api/jobs/ returned the real seeded jobs
  through the proxy, and ?status=RUNNING filtered correctly. Local build is
  169ms, 69 kB gzipped.

  make dev explicitly stops the frontend container, because it publishes 5173
  and Vite wants the same port — otherwise you'd get a confusing bind failure.
  Override the proxy target with VITE_API_PROXY_TARGET if you ever point it
  elsewhere.

  Version alignment

  I moved everything to Node 24: .nvmrc → 24, and the Dockerfile's build stage →
  node:24-alpine. Reasoning: Node 24 is the current active LTS (22 is in
  maintenance), it's what you installed, and keeping .nvmrc equal to the image
  version means local dev and the container build run the same runtime rather
  than two that merely both satisfy the engine range. Rebuilt and re-verified
  the container stack on it — frontend 200, API through nginx 200.

  Your other nvm versions (18, 19, 21) all fall outside ^20.19 || >=22.12, which
  is why nvm use reading .nvmrc matters here.

  Commit for review

  Two things I did to make the split honest

  Every commit builds independently. App.tsx in the scaffold commit is a plain
  shell with no API import; the client commit adds the import along with the
  module it needs. Otherwise be5a531 would reference a file that doesn't exist
  yet and break git bisect. Verified: tsc -b --force passes at all three
  frontend commits.

  Ordering follows dependencies. make dev runs docker compose stop frontend, so
  it ships in the same commit that defines that service — not earlier, where it
  would fail with "no such service."

  Each message also states why rather than restating the diff: why
  erasableSyntaxOnly forced the ApiError shape, why listen [::]:80 would be the
  wrong fix for the nginx probe, why the relative /api path avoids baking a
  deploy target into the bundle.

❯ show me the diff of ac1bc8c                                                   

  Ran 3 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ That's the complete commit. A short orientation, plus three things in it I'd
  expect a reviewer to push on — worth raising before you have to find them.

  What to read closely

  parseErrorBody is the load-bearing part. DRF returns {"detail": "Not found."}
  for routing failures but {"name": ["This field may not be blank."]} for
  validation, and a value can be a bare string (our ?status= error) or a list.
  Normalising both into one ApiError means the UI has a single error type to
  handle, and keeps fieldErrors available so the create form can show the
  message against the name input rather than in a generic banner.

  Three things I'd tighten

  1. cursor silently overrides status and pageSize. If you call listJobs({ 
  status: 'RUNNING', cursor: someUrl }), the status is ignored — correctly,
  since the cursor URL already carries the filter that produced it, but
  silently. The type should make that impossible rather than relying on the
  comment:

  export type ListJobsOptions =
    | { status?: StatusType | null; pageSize?: number }
    | { cursor: string }

  2. return undefined as T for 204 is an unsound cast. It's safe as called —
  only deleteJob hits a 204, and it's typed Promise<void> — but nothing stops
  someone writing request<Job>(...) against a 204 endpoint and getting undefined
  typed as Job. A separate requestNoContent helper would remove the lie.

  3. The bare catch {} discards the original fetch error. The message is
  friendlier, but a DNS failure and a TLS failure now look identical in the
  console. Passing { cause } through would keep the diagnostic without changing
  what the user sees.

  None of these are bugs today. (1) is the one I'd actually fix, since it's a
  footgun that gets more likely once the UI wires up filter tabs and pagination
  — which is exactly the next commit. Want me to fold that union type in as I
  build the table, or leave it?

⏺ Now verifying the union type actually rejects the invalid combination, rather

⏺ All four valid shapes pass; the non-literal combination is caught by the never
  members — which is exactly why they're there rather than relying on excess
  property checking. Verifying the stack still works, then committing:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ All three fixed in f3ccacf, one file, +46/−23.

  What changed

  listJobs takes an exclusive union. Either { status?, pageSize? } or { cursor }
  — never both. Verified it rejects the invalid combination in both forms:

  listJobs({ status: 'RUNNING', cursor: url })     → TS2322
  const bad: ListJobsOptions = sneaky               → TS2322

  The second case is why the never members are there. Excess property checking
  only fires on fresh object literals, so a combination assembled in a variable
  would have slipped through a plain union. All three valid shapes still
  compile.

  204s go through requestNoContent. send() now holds the shared
  fetch-and-translate-errors logic; request<T>() parses a body,
  requestNoContent() doesn't. The undefined as T cast is gone, so nothing can
  ask for a typed body from a response that has none.

  Network failures carry cause. catch (cause) → new ApiError(..., { cause }), so
  the underlying TypeError survives for debugging while the user still sees
  "Could not reach the server."

  One honest gap

  I verified these at the type level and confirmed the stack still serves
  (frontend 200, API 200), but the deleteJob and error-parsing runtime paths
  aren't exercised yet — the placeholder UI only lists. They're hard to unit
  test as written, because API_ROOT is a relative /api and fetch can't resolve a
  relative URL outside a document.

  That resolves itself with the UI: the table will exercise delete and the
  create form will exercise fieldErrors. If you want them covered by tests
  rather than by use, React Testing Library with a mocked fetch is the stretch
  goal for frontend unit tests, and I'd point it at client.ts first since it's
  the piece with real branching.

  The four "assumed" ones aren't at risk — they're small and uncontroversial,
  and I'll build them. I'm flagging them because you asked whether the plan
  covered the requirements, and strictly it didn't; it covered the design forks.

  The one that needs a decision: dynamic updates

  This is where my decisions collide. With cursor pagination and a server-side
  status filter, "update the UI after a mutation" isn't obvious:

  - Create while filtered to COMPLETED — the new job is PENDING, so it doesn't
    belong in the current view. Adding the row would be wrong; not adding it
    looks broken.
  - Change status while filtered — the row may no longer match the filter and
    should leave the list.
  - Delete the last row on a page — the page becomes empty while later pages
    still exist.

  My recommendation: refetch the current page after every mutation, and make 
  create always land somewhere visible. Concretely — after a create, reset to
  the first page and clear the status filter if it would hide a PENDING job;
  after a status change or delete, refetch the page currently in view, stepping
  back a page if it comes back empty and a previous cursor exists.

  The reasoning is that under keyset pagination plus filtering, the server is 
  the authority on what belongs on a page. Reproducing that client-side means
  writing a second implementation of the same rules that will drift. A refetch
  is 25 rows against a query we measured at 0.125 ms, so it's imperceptible, and
  it makes the displayed page true by construction rather than by careful
  bookkeeping.

  That also satisfies the required Playwright test directly — "creating a new
  job and verifying it appears in the list with the correct initial status" only
  passes reliably if create guarantees visibility.

  The alternative is optimistic updates: mutate local state immediately,
  reconcile later. Snappier on a slow link, but it means duplicating the filter
  and ordering logic and handling rollback on failure. I'd skip it and note the
  trade in the README.

  One small fork while I'm here

  The status dropdown will offer all four states. Selecting the state a job is
  already in would append a duplicate history entry — harmless and arguably
  legitimate, but it looks like a bug in the history timeline. I'd treat it as a
  client-side no-op: keep all four selectable, send nothing if it matches the
  current status.

  Also worth noting

  The requirement says "a list of all jobs," and we paginate. That's deliberate
  and sanctioned by requirement 5's millions-of-jobs framing, and I'll say so in
  the README. It won't look truncated during review either — the 10 seeded jobs
  fit on one page of 25.

  Confirm the refetch approach and the no-op dropdown behavior, and I'll build
  the table slice.

✻ Churned for 1m 19s · done 6:57 PM

❯ On the no-op dropdown -- what do platforms like EC2 do today? Let's match     
  their UX for the time being, then come back to this later if necessary. On    
  the refresh on mutation -- let's do this for now but note the tradeoff in a   
  code comment by marking non-hard refresh as a future TODO                     

⏺ Good question to ask, and it changes my recommendation.

  What EC2 actually does

  The AWS console doesn't offer "set status to X" at all. It offers valid 
  actions as verbs — the Instance state menu lists Start instance, Stop 
  instance, Reboot instance, Terminate instance — and greys out the actions that
  don't apply to the current state. A running instance has "Start instance"
  disabled; a stopped one has "Stop instance" disabled. The state itself is a
  read-only column. Actions are also disabled while an instance is
  mid-transition, until it settles.

  So the console's answer to my question is: disable the inapplicable option, 
  don't silently ignore a click. That's strictly better than my proposed no-op,
  because a control that accepts a click and does nothing reads as broken,
  whereas a disabled option tells you why up front.

  Matching that, adapted to a requirement that explicitly asks for target states
  rather than verbs:

  - All four states appear in the dropdown, so "any of the defined states" is
    satisfiable
  - The option matching the current status is disabled and marked as current
  - The whole control is disabled while a request for that row is in flight

  That removes the need for a no-op path entirely — the invalid selection can't
  be made.

  Worth flagging: I'm describing the console from knowledge of it rather than
  having just checked it. The structural point I'm confident about is
  actions-with-invalid-options-disabled; if you want the verb-based framing
  instead of target states, that's a bigger departure from the PDF's wording and
  worth deciding deliberately.

  Building the first slice now — tokens, status badge, table, filter tabs, and
  the data hook. Read-only; mutations come next.

⏺ Both commits staged for your review. The fix commit's full diff is above; the
  feature commit is 14 files, +738/−36, all of which you saw as I wrote them.

  Commit A — fix(frontend): keep pagination working behind the nginx proxy

  Two faults in how requests reach Django through nginx, both invisible until a
  second page existed — the seeded data fit on one page, so `next` was always
  null in the UI.

  nginx forwarded `Host $host`, which drops the port. DRF builds cursor links
  from that header, so "next" pointed at http://localhost/ — port 80, where
  nothing listens — and following it failed as an unreachable server. It now
  forwards $http_host, which keeps the port.

  Django also rejected any Host not in ALLOWED_HOSTS, and a client inside the
  compose network reaches the app as `frontend`. That would have failed the
  containerised E2E run outright; `frontend` is now allowed.

  The client additionally resolves cursor links against its own origin, using
  only their path and query, so paging no longer depends on what host Django
  believed it was serving. Redundant given the two fixes above; it exists so a
  future proxy change cannot silently break paging again.

  Verified in headless Chromium against 30 jobs: page 1 → Next → page 2, Next
  disabled on the last page, Previous restores page 1.

  Commit B — feat(frontend): add the job table with status filter and pagination

  First, read-only slice of the dashboard, modelled on the AWS console: a dense
  table with status badges, status tabs, and Refresh and Previous/Next 
  top-right.

  useJobs derives loading and error state from which request last settled,
  rather than setting them when a request starts. That keeps every state update
  inside an async callback, and it fixed a real bug in the first draft: after a
  failed filter change, the previous filter's rows stayed on screen under the
  newly selected tab, beside the error, where they read as belonging to it. Rows
  are now tied to the view that produced them — kept, dimmed, only while their
  replacement loads.

  The tbody's aria-busy reflects that state, and doubles as the signal a test
  must wait on: asserting immediately after an action observes the previous
  view's rows.

  Filtering is server-side and the tabs carry no counts, since a count per
  status would need the COUNT(*) that keyset pagination exists to avoid. Badges
  carry their label as text, never colour alone. Errors surface in an aria-live
  region that is always mounted, because a region inserted together with its
  content is not reliably announced.

  A TODO in useJobs records the refetch-on-mutation tradeoff: correct by
  construction, at one round trip per mutation, with optimistic updates as the
  upgrade path.

  Verified

  - tsc clean, zero lint warnings (was two), production build 70.8 kB gzipped
  - In real Chromium: pagination both directions, Next/Previous disabled at the
    ends, filter change from page 2 resets to page 1 and shows only matching
    rows — stable across three runs
  - Light and dark themes; no horizontal page scroll at 420px

  One thing needing your approval — a Django file

  The identical timestamps are a bug in seed_jobs.py, and its comment is false.
  It claims to stagger history "so created_at ordering is meaningful," but only
  the status entries are backdated; created_at is auto_now_add, so every job
  lands in the same minute.

  The fix is seed-only: after recording a job's statuses, set created_at to its
  first status timestamp and updated_at to its last, via a queryset .update() —
  which bypasses auto_now_add/auto_now. No change to the model or to
  record_status(). Want it?

  Note the dev database currently holds 30 jobs so pagination has something to
  page through; make seed puts it back to 10.

  Approve A and B and I'll commit them, then move on to slice 2 — the create
  form, the inline status control with the current state disabled, and delete
  with confirmation.

⏺ I've confirmed this is a DRF cursor-pagination bug: when a page becomes empty
  after a delete, its "previous" link anchors incorrectly and hides a row on the
  following page. I'll fix this by tracking a history stack of cursors we've
  actually visited instead of trusting DRF's previous link, keeping cursor and
  history in one synced state object.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Backend suite still green, 27 of 27. Two commits staged for review. Commit C's
  full diff is above; commit D is slice 2.

  Commit C — fix(jobs): give seeded jobs distinct, realistic timestamps

  seed_jobs claimed to stagger history "so created_at ordering is meaningful",
  but only the status entries were backdated. created_at is auto_now_add and
  updated_at auto_now, so every job was stamped with the moment of seeding and
  the dashboard's Created and Updated columns showed one identical time.

  Each job now starts 30 minutes after the previous one, its transitions
  follow 5 minutes apart, and a queryset update — which bypasses auto_now_add
  and auto_now — sets created_at to the start of its history and updated_at to
  its newest transition. Seeding only; the model and record_status() are
  untouched, and the current_status invariant still holds for every job.

  The default count rises to 40, more than one page at the API's default page
  size of 25, so a freshly seeded dashboard has something to page through.

  Commit D — feat(frontend): create, change status and delete jobs

  18 files. The ones worth reading closely are useJobs.ts (rewritten),
  useJobMutations.ts, CreateJobForm.tsx, JobActions.tsx, and ConfirmDialog.tsx.

  Second slice of the dashboard, covering the remaining section 2 requirements.

  Create: a form in the header, as the console's primary action. Validation
  runs on submit rather than per keystroke, then re-runs as the user edits, so
  an untouched field is not flagged but a fixed one clears immediately. The
  button stays enabled when the field is empty — a disabled button cannot say
  why. Server field errors from ApiError appear against the input. The length
  limit is enforced by maxLength, which also truncates pastes, so it needs no
  message.

  Change status: follows the AWS console's instance-state menu. Every state is
  listed, the current one disabled and marked "(current)", so a duplicate
  history entry cannot be requested rather than being silently dropped. The
  menu resets to its prompt after each choice, since it is a command; the
  status column shows the value.

  Delete: confirmed in a native <dialog>, which provides focus trapping, inert
  page content and Escape handling. Cancel comes first so it takes initial
  focus, and Enter on an unexpected dialog is never the destructive choice.

  In-flight state is per job, so one row's request locks only that row. Every
  mutation refetches the page in view, as recorded in the TODO in useJobs. A
  create returns to the first page and clears a filter that would hide the new
  job, so the user always sees what they made.

  Paging back now replays our own cursor history instead of following DRF's
  `previous` link. The two agree for a populated page, but not for one a delete
  has just emptied: DRF then anchors `previous` on the cursor position, which
  is the last row of the page before, and reverse paging excludes its anchor —
  verified to return 24 rows with the boundary job stranded alone on the next
  page. With history, deleting the lone row on page 3 of 51 lands on a page
  identical to the original page 2, and Previous from there reproduces page 1.

  ErrorBanner becomes Banner with an error or success tone, since mutations
  also report success. Shared button and field styles move into
  controls.module.css.

  Verified in Chromium: empty and whitespace names blocked with no request sent;
  create lands on top as Pending, including from a filter that excluded it; the
  current status option disabled; a status change under a filter removes the
  row from view; the dialog opens on Cancel, Escape keeps the job and confirm
  deletes it; a forced 400 shows the server's message under the field; a
  forced network failure shows the error banner and re-enables the row.

  One known gap

  After you confirm a delete, the dialog returns focus to the Delete button —
  which is then removed along with its row, so focus falls back to <body>.
  Keyboard users lose their place. The fix is to move focus to the table or the
  next row after a delete. It's small, but it's a behavior change, so I'd rather
  do it deliberately than slip it in here. Want it in this commit or as a
  follow-up?

  Approve C and D and I'll commit, then build slice 3: the /jobs/:id detail
  route with the status-history timeline.

✻ Sautéed for 43s · done 8:45 PM

❯ Commits look good so far. Proceed                                             

⏺ Starting on the evaluation gate: Playwright in make test. The design first,
  since it has a few real choices:

  - TypeScript Playwright Test in its own e2e/ package, matching the frontend's
    language, and running in Microsoft's Playwright image as a Compose service
    behind a test profile — so make up never starts it, and the evaluator needs
    nothing beyond Docker.
  - It targets http://frontend over the Compose network — the path the
    ALLOWED_HOSTS fix made work.
  - Tests don't depend on seed data. Each one creates uniquely named jobs and
    deletes them afterwards through the API, so the suite passes on an empty
    database (the evaluator's case) and on your populated one alike.
  - One worker. The tests share a live database and the list is ordered by
    creation time, so parallel workers could interleave in ways that make a
    correct test fail. The suite is small; determinism is worth more than the
    seconds.
  - No retries. A flaky test should fail loudly rather than pass on its second
    try.

  Checking versions so the npm package matches the browser image exactly:

⏺ The evaluation gate is done: make test now runs the Playwright suite. It
  passes from a clean slate and fails when the app breaks. Two commits are ready
  for your review. E is a small frontend fix; F adds the Playwright suite. Full
  diffs are above except the e2e/ source files, which you saw as I wrote them.

  Commit E — fix(frontend): expose the list's loading state even when it is 
  empty

  aria-busy lived on the table's <tbody>, which does not exist while the list is
  empty: the table renders an empty-state paragraph instead. Nothing announced
  loading on the first load of an empty database, and anything waiting on that
  signal — the end-to-end suite included — would wait forever there.

  JobTable now wraps both the table and its empty state in a labelled "Jobs"
  region that carries aria-busy, so the signal exists in every state. The
  dimming of stale rows keys off the region instead of the tbody.

  Commit F — test(e2e): run a Playwright suite as part of make test

  make test now does what the brief defines it as doing: run Playwright E2E
  tests. Backend unit tests still run first, since they are fast and pinpoint a
  broken model or view directly rather than as a browser timeout.

  The suite runs in Microsoft's Playwright image as a compose service behind a
  `test` profile, so the evaluator needs nothing but Docker and `make up` never
  starts it. It reaches the app as `frontend` over the compose network. The
  image tag and the @playwright/test version are pinned together, since the
  image's browsers are built for one release.

  Six tests: the two flows the brief names (create shows Pending; a status
  change updates and appends a history entry, checked through the API), empty-
  name validation with no request sent, delete confirmation, server-side
  filtering, and pagination across a page boundary.

  Tests share the live database, so each names its jobs with a per-test token
  and deletes them through the API on teardown — even when the test fails. The
  suite passes on an empty database and a populated one alike, and leaves no
  trace: 40 seeded jobs untouched after five consecutive runs. One worker and
  no retries: ordering assertions on a shared list are not safe in parallel,
  and a flaky test should fail rather than pass on its second attempt.

  Mutation-checked. Starting new jobs RUNNING fails the Pending test; a PATCH
  that records nothing fails the status and filter tests; dropping the refresh
  after a mutation fails the status and delete tests. That check caught the
  suite's own bug: assertions on a row's text matched the status menu's option
  labels, so "row contains Pending" held for a job in any state. Status is now
  asserted on the status cell exactly.

  Verified from `make clean`: 27 backend tests, then 6 E2E tests, exit code 0;
  exit code 2 when a test fails.

  Worth knowing

  The first make test takes about 90 seconds. That's a one-time pull and build
  of the Playwright image; later runs take seconds. It's worth a line in the
  README so the evaluator doesn't assume it's hung.

  My earlier ad-hoc browser scripts would not have caught the vacuous assertion.
  They used the same row-text pattern. Mutation testing is the only thing that
  caught it, which is a good argument for running it on any suite where passing
  is the whole point.

  Approve E and F and I'll commit them. The README is next, and it needs one
  thing only you can supply: how much time you've spent on this, which the PDF
  requires. I'll draft everything else, including the performance section with
  the measured numbers and the AI-usage writeup, which I can build from this
  session's actual iteration: the bugs caught, the decisions you pushed back on,
  and the mutation-testing catch above.
