❯ Walk me through the Playwright tests -- what flow does "the two flows the     
  brief names" test?                                                            
  ⎿  ⧉ Selected 1 lines from e2e/tests/jobs.spec.ts in Visual Studio Code

⏺ The describe block at e2e/tests/jobs.spec.ts:8 groups two tests, one for each
  example flow in section 3 of the PDF. That section says:

  ▎ Include at least one End-to-End (E2E) test using Playwright. This test 
  ▎ should cover a critical user flow, such as:
  ▎ ■ Creating a new job and verifying it appears in the list with the correct 
  ▎ initial status.
  ▎ ■ Updating a job's status to a different available status and verifying the 
  ▎ change.

  I implemented both. Before each test, beforeEach loads / and waits for the
  list to finish loading.

  Test 1 — creating a job shows the initial Pending status (jobs.spec.ts:9)

  1. Get a unique name. jobName('Fluid Dynamics Simulation') returns something
     like Fluid Dynamics Simulation [e2e-3f9a…-lx2k]. The token lets the test
     find its own row in a database that may hold anything else, and lets
     teardown delete the job afterwards.
  2. Create it through the form, as a user would. createJobViaForm types into
     the "New job" input, clicks Create job, and waits for the list to reload.
  3. Assert the status cell reads exactly Pending. This is the check I fixed
     after mutation testing. It targets the status cell, not the row, because
     the row also contains the status menu, whose options list every status.
  4. Assert the confirmation banner says Created "<name>".
  5. Reload the page and assert Pending again. Without this step the test could
     pass on state held only in React. A job that's still Pending after a full
     reload came from the server.

  Catches: the backend starting jobs in the wrong state (verified by the M1
  mutation), the form not submitting, the list not refreshing, or the job not
  being persisted.

  Test 2 — changing a status updates it and records a new entry 
  (jobs.spec.ts:23)

  1. Create a job through the form, as in test 1. It starts Pending.
  2. Choose RUNNING from that row's menu, found by its accessible label Change 
     status of <name>, then wait for the list to reload.
  3. Assert the status cell reads exactly Running.
  4. Assert that Running (current) is now the disabled option. This checks the
     EC2-style behavior we agreed on: you can't request the state the job is
     already in.
  5. Reload and assert Running again, confirming the change persisted
     server-side.
  6. Check the history through the API. The test reads the job's ID from the
     first cell, calls GET /api/jobs/<id>/, and asserts status_history is
     exactly ['RUNNING', 'PENDING'], newest first.

  Step 6 is there because the PDF says a status change "should trigger the
  creation of a new JobStatus entry on the backend." The UI alone can't show
  that difference. A backend that overwrote the status in place would display
  Running just the same. Only the history reveals whether an entry was appended.

  Catches: a PATCH that doesn't record anything (verified by M3), a missing
  refresh after the change (verified by M2), overwriting instead of appending,
  and the current-state option not being disabled.

  A naming problem you've surfaced

  "The two flows the brief names" only makes sense to someone who has read the
  PDF. In a test report it tells a reviewer nothing about what's being tested.
  It describes where the tests came from, not what they check.

  I'd rename it to job lifecycle and keep the link to the brief as a one-line
  comment above the block. The test titles themselves already describe the
  behavior.

  Should I make that rename part of commit F before committing?

  GitHub still has the PDF

  origin/main on GitHub is still the old Init repo commit. The repo is private,
  so the PDF hasn't been exposed publicly. Your local history and GitHub's now
  share no commits, so the first push will have to overwrite main whichever way
  you go. I haven't pushed anything. You have two options:

  1. Force-push, with git push --force origin main. This replaces GitHub's main,
     but GitHub can keep the orphaned commit reachable by its hash for a while.
  2. Delete and recreate the GitHub repo, then push normally. This is the only
     way to be certain the PDF is gone from GitHub, and costs nothing here,
     since that one commit is all the repo holds.

  I'd recommend option 2 if you'll make the repo public for your submission.
  Option 1 is fine if it stays private. Until you decide, avoid git fetch: it
  would pull the old commit, and the PDF with it, back into your local repo as
  origin/main.

  If you typed a URL like /jobs/5, you'll have seen the job list, which makes it
  look like navigation quietly failed. That comes from nginx's single-page-app
  fallback. Unknown paths serve index.html so that a client-side router can
  handle them. With no router, the app renders the only view it has.

  Two things I should own:

  - getJob and JobDetail are unused code. I added them with the API client in
    anticipation of this page, which runs against your no-cruft rule. They'll
    either get used in the next slice or should be removed.
  - The README is accurate: "Known limitations" says there's no detail page yet.
    My last message did too, but I never explicitly pointed out that those
    client functions were dead ends.

  Building it

  This is slice 3, which would close two stretch goals at once: status history 
  view and details page with a distinct URL. One decision affects the design, so
  I'd rather settle it with you first.

  Should the list's filter and page live in the URL? Right now they're React
  state. Once there's a second page, opening a job and pressing Back would reset
  the list to "All", page 1, which is annoying in a console you use to triage
  failures. Putting the filter in the query string (/?status=FAILED) fixes that
  and makes filtered views shareable. The cursor could go there too, but those
  URLs would be long and opaque. My recommendation is to keep the filter in the
  URL and the page in history state, so Back returns you to the same page but a
  shared link opens page 1.

  The rest follows the console model: job names in the table become links to
  /jobs/:id; the detail page shows the job's status, created and updated times,
  the same status menu and delete action as the list, and a newest-first history
  timeline; an unknown ID shows a proper "not found" state rather than an
  error.

  It adds one dependency, react-router, and new Playwright coverage for opening
  a job, the history after a status change, and Back restoring the filter. Shall
  I go ahead with that?

✻ Churned for 24s · done 9:25 PM

❯ Yes, put the filter in the query string. Proceed as you propose with          
  implementing the detail page                                                  

⏺ Before building, here's the design, including two choices I've made:

  - Filter in the query string (/?status=FAILED), as you decided. An invalid
    value in a hand-edited URL is treated as "All" and cleaned out of the
    address bar.
  - Filter changes replace the history entry rather than pushing a new one. That
    way Back means "leave this screen", not "undo my last tab click". That's
    the console behavior, and it's also how Previous/Next already work. Say if
    you'd rather each filter click be its own Back step.
  - Page position lives in history state, so Back from a job returns you to the
    same page of the list, while a shared link opens page 1. The position
    records which filter it belongs to, so it can never be applied under the
    wrong one.
  - Job names become links to /jobs/:id. The detail page shows a breadcrumb back
    to the list, the job's status, ID and timestamps, the same status menu and
    Delete as the list, and a newest-first history. Deleting from the detail
    page returns you to the list with the confirmation notice.
  - An unknown or malformed ID gets a proper not-found state, as does any
    unknown path.

  Checking the router version first:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The two commits, with proposed messages:

  Commit G: feat(frontend): add a page per job, and keep the filter in the URL

  20 files, +752/−76. The ones to read closely are useJobs.ts,
  JobDetailPage.tsx, and lib/navigation.ts.

  Each job now has its own page at /jobs/:id, reached from its name in the
  list: a breadcrumb back, its status, ID and timestamps, the same status menu
  and Delete as the list, and its status history as a newest-first timeline.
  This covers two stretch goals, the history view and a details page with a
  distinct URL, and puts getJob() and the JobDetail type to use — both had
  been written with the API client and left unused until now.

  The list's filter moves into the query string, so a filtered view can be
  bookmarked and shared; an unrecognised value falls back to all jobs and is
  removed from the URL. The page position moves into history state, so Back
  from a job returns to the same page of the list while a shared link opens
  page one. Both are written with replace, so Back leaves the list rather than
  undoing filter clicks and page turns one at a time. A stored position
  records the filter it was made under and is ignored under any other, since a
  cursor is meaningless outside the filter that produced it.

  The job page carries the list view it came from, so its breadcrumb restores
  the same filter and page. Deleting from it returns to that view, replacing
  the dead page in history so Back cannot revisit it, and hands the "Deleted"
  notice over for the list to show. Everything read from history state is
  validated, since it survives reloads and may predate the current build.

  A 404 gets a not-found page rather than an error with a pointless Retry, as
  do malformed IDs and unknown paths. Pages set the tab title.

  Adds react-router 8.4, about 17 KB gzipped. It requires Node >= 22.22, which
  the Node 24 build image and .nvmrc satisfy.

  Commit H: test(e2e): cover the job page and the list's URL state

  2 files, +146.

  Six tests: opening a job shows its details and history; a status change on
  its page appends to the history; Back and the breadcrumb both restore the
  list's filter and page; deleting from its page returns to the list with a
  confirmation, and Back does not revisit the dead page; unknown jobs and paths
  get a not-found page; the filter is read from and written to the URL.

  Mutation-checked: history shown oldest-first fails the two history tests; a
  delete that pushes instead of replacing fails the delete test; ignoring the
  stored position fails the restore test. That last check first reported a
  false pass — the mutation did not compile, the previous build kept serving,
  and the harness had discarded the build output. It now fails loudly when a
  mutated build breaks.

  12 of 12 pass from a clean slate and across five consecutive runs, leaving
  no jobs behind.

  One thing I'd flag in G

  useJobs.ts has grown to about 250 lines, and it now owns four things:
  fetching, cursor history, URL sync and history-state sync. Every piece is
  justified and tested, but it's the file a reviewer would find hardest to
  follow. If you'd like, I can split the URL and history-state handling into its
  own useListLocation hook as a separate refactor commit after these land, so
  the refactor is reviewable on its own and not tangled into the feature.

  Approve G and H and I'll commit them. The README stays untracked until you've
  finished editing it.

  The first row is the problem. You rewrote history specifically to get the
  assignment out of the repo, and committing this file would put its full text
  straight back in, just as text rather than as a PDF.

  It's also the wrong format for the reader

  - It's raw JSONL. Each line is an internal event record: permission modes,
    file snapshots, system reminders and base64 images mixed in with the
    conversation. An evaluator who opens it sees 8 MB of JSON, not the
    back-and-forth they want to read.
  - It's incomplete. The session is still running, so anything committed now
    would be missing everything from this point on.
  - The PDF asks for a link. "Please include a screen recording or transcript
    link," so a repo file isn't what's being asked for anyway.

  What I'd suggest

  1. At the end of the session, run /export in Claude Code. It writes a readable
     text version of the conversation. I haven't checked exactly which tool
     output it includes, so we'd check the result the same way I checked this
     file.

     paths if you like. I'd show you what I removed.

     docs/ai-transcript.md and link it from the README's AI section.
